===== Page 7 =====
FOREWORD

You just bought or bought a cartridge
ASSEMBLEUR MO6.

This module is a new asset for your computer.

With this cartridge ‘‘Language Module 6899' you will be able to write programs in a language as close to the machine as possible.

Before you go further, you need to understand that the "heart" or "brain" of your computer is a microprocess-
seur 6809.

6899 includes only one language, his language is machine language.

Machine language consists of a succession of binary codes, expressed in hexadecimal, whose grouping, in packages of one, two, three, four or five values, is called instruction. The succession of instructions constitutes the programme machine or programme object.

Programs written in advanced languages (BASIC, LOGO...) to be executable, i.e. understandable by 6809, must be translated into machine program.

Two solutions can be chosen for this task:

1 - Translating the advanced program, in full, before running it machine. This work is carried out by another program called a compiler. It is then said that the program is compiled and then executed.

2 - Translate the line-by-line program or instruction by instruction before machine execution of the corresponding binary codes.

It is then said that the advanced language is interpreted.

BASIC, the LOGO of MO6 are interpreted languages.

The first solution involves translation processing before the program is run. But once this work is done, a new execution no longer requires compilation.

The second solution requires a translation of each instruction before it is executed, resulting in a reduced speed of execution.

It is mainly the improvement of this parameter that your ASSEMBLEUR proposes.


===== Page 8 =====
The speed of execution of an instruction is an important parameter. The increase in the speed of execution will allow you to write programs (e.g. animation) that would have no meaning, in an interpreted language, because their execution would be too slow. The role of ASSEMBLEUR is to translate the mnemonic representation of the instructions into their binary equivalent which may have between one and five bytes. An byte is an eight-bit word. This resulting binary code, called object program, is directly executable by the microprocessor. The program written in mnemonic is called source program.

This task, which is called "assembly", is carried out by the ASSEMBLEUR.

The ROM cartridge also contains an EDITOR to write source programs, as well as a MONITOR that will allow you to check the operation of the object program before running or calling it, possibly under control of another language.

The assembly language stays very close to the machine. It depends on the limits of the microprocessor used. That is why we believe that it is necessary to understand the internal structure and the method of addressing the microprocessor used to program efficiently in the assembly.

This book, which is a basic manual, not a course on assembler, describes in detail the various orders at your disposal.

Each command is applied, for example, to a standard program used throughout the book.

In the second part you will find the instructions and the address mode of the 6809. You will also find the entry points of the MO6 system monitor.

Then you will find the entry points of the system extramonitor of the MO6, as well as the entry points of the gate array mode page circuit of the MO6.

GOOD PROGRAMMING (in assembler!!).


===== Page 9 =====
CHAPITRE 1
ORGANIZATION OF THE CARTRIDGE
ASSEMBLEUR

The Assembly cartridge processes source programs written in assembly language 6809. It translates the source instructions into an object program compatible with the 6899 microprocessor.

The MENU provides access to five blocks of programs that are:
EDITOR, MONITOR, FILE MANAGEMENT, SETTING
PRINTER, BACK IN THE MAIN MENU.
Their entry points through the keyboard are:

EDITOR —{1] EDIT

(MONITOR)

DIRECTORY

COPY

[GESTION DE FICHIERS |=s——————_{5]_ RENAME

=H IL FORMAT

RÉGLAGE DE L’IMPRIMANTE | Hi
89 or 49 COLUMNS columns [MENU PRINCIPAL }

The EDIT command line allows access to the editor and the ASSEMBLEUR function (resident in the editor). The MONITOR command line allows access to the monitor.


===== Page 10 =====
The FILE MANAGEMENT block consists of five routines accessible by the MENU. — DIRECTORY (disc catalogue)

— COPY (copying a file) — RENAME (suming a file) — KILL (exface a file)

— FORMAT (initializes a disc)

The PRINTER COLUMNS command line allows to declare the width of the listing on the printer.

The QUIT line allows the return to the main menu of the machine.

1.2 Menu

The choice in MENU is as follows:

EDIT EI COPY FORMAT
MONITOR RENAME PRINTER COLUMNS
DIRECTORY HE KILL EI QUIT

The call to a MENU command is made by typing the corresponding number. At the bottom of the screen, the red line is the comment area of MENU, intended to receive error messages or questions to the user. Example: ‘‘Are you sure Y/N?'

(Are you sure O/N?)

(To validate file deletion)

validates the MENU commands. allows the cancellation of an order.

COPY, RENAME, KILL require at least one file descriptor before the command is validated. The presence of a descriptor is not required to enter MONITOR and EDIT.

The figure corresponding to your choice on MENU shows a blue square (cursor), at the beginning of a yellow zone delimiting the format (i.e. length) of the file descriptor.

EDIT and MONITOR call their specific screen.

EDIT copies the input file descriptor (possibly completed suffix and device) on the editor command line.

===== Page 13 =====
1.3 File Descriptor

The general syntax of file descriptors is: <file descriptor > = < device >: <filename >

As <peripheral> one can have:

: for the program recorder (LEP)

: for the first disk drive

: for the second disk drive

: for the third disk drive

: for the fourth disk drive

: for virtual diskette (RAM DISK)

By default, (if <peripheral> is missing in < file descriptor>) the system takes SARA as a device, if no disk drive is present.

If a disk drive is present, drive 9 is taken by default.

wn oo

File name: the <filename> consists of the name itself and an optional suffix.

<filename> = <name>. <sufficient>

<name> is composed of no more than one to eight characters. <suffixe> is optional and has one to three characters.

<name> and <suffixe> must be separated by a point.

By default (in the absence of another suffix), the system adds .ASM for source files

BIN for object files

Examples of file descriptors:

2: ASSEMBLY.BIN 1: TEST.MO6

0 : TRAIN.BUS C : BETTING.V

Note: Double-sided drives are managed as two single-sided drives. Thus diskettes 0 and 1 are assigned to the internal drive and 2 and 3 to the external drive.

CHAPITRE 2
L’EDITEUR

The editor allows the writing and assembly of a source program. It allows, in the editing phase, correction of errors, insertion of new lines, search for labels, transfer of program blocks to/from a device, etc.

The editor uses the entire screen and accepts input source program lines or commands. It generates output, after assembly, the object program. The program lines are entered in EDITION mode. The commands are entered in EDITOR ORDERS mode.

The screen under editor control is divided into three zones:

Editing area

Editor command line

Comment line

A - EDITION field: where full screen editor is available (from line @ to line 23, blue characters on black background). The program part displayed on the screen is a window on the source program residing in the memory.

Each source program line consists of four fields: A tag (or character (*) for comment lines), an operation code, an operand and a comment.

• Label) (Comments)
9 6 7 13 14 20 22 39

18


===== Page 18 =====
The label field

The label field is the first field of a source program line. It is defined between columns @ and 6 and can take one of the following forms:

1. An asterisk (*) placed at the top of the label field means that the rest of the source program line is a comment. The comments are ignored by the assembler, and are only present in the source program to inform the user.

2. A character at the top of the line means that it has a label. The characters are capital letters from A to Z and digits from 9 to 9. Special characters: point (.), dollar ($), etc. are not allowed. The labels consist of one to six characters. The first must be an alphabetical character. Some labels are reserved for assembly and should not be used in the label field. If this rule is not respected, an error would be generated. The reserved labels are A, B, D, X, Y, CC, DP, PC, S, U which represent the registers
du 6899.

3. A space at the head of the source line indicates that the label field is empty. The line then did not label and cannot be a comment. In this case, the label field can be reduced to a space, the operation code field then taking the place of the label field.

A label may only be present once in the label field, except when used with the SET Assembly Directive. If a label is used several times, each passage on this label will be reported by the ‘Multiple defined symbol' error.

The field code operation

The operation code field is located after the label field between columns 7 and 13. It must be preceded by at least one space and contain the mnemonic of an instruction 6899 or an assembly directive. It consists only of capital letters from A to Z. The content of the operation code field can therefore be of two types: — Mnemonics: corresponding to the machine instructions of the 6899 microprocessor.

19


===== Page 19 =====
— Assembly directives: operational codes recognised by the assembly body, acting instead on the assembly process and not translated into object programme.

The field operand

The operating field follows the operation code field. It lies between columns 14 and 26 but can be extended up to column 39. In all cases, a space should be used as a separator between the operand field and the comment field. The interpretation of the operand field depends on the content of the operation code field. It may contain a label, symbol, expression, number or combination of the four. It is also used to specify the address mode when the operation code field contains a mnemonic.

Labels and symbols

The labels and symbols consist of not more than six characters and begin with a letter. An integer value between 8 and 65535 (sixteen bits) is associated with them and replaces them in the evaluation of an expression that contains the specified labels or symbols.

Some symbols are specific and can only be used in expressions. These are:

(*) (asterisk) or (.) (point), which represents the current value of the program counter (PC).

ENDMEM, which defines the end of user memory.

Expressions

The expressions consist of a series of symbols, numbers, logical or arithmetic operators and parentheses. The expressions in the field operand are used to specify a value. The rules of logic and arithmetic apply to expressions.

Example:
ENDMEM - $4000 DEPART + $30 * < -8

... are expressions.

20


===== Page 20 =====
Operators

Operators in an expression can be of two types: logical or arithmetic, and they are ranked from one to five. Higher level operations will be performed prior to lower level operations. Operations at the same hierarchical level will be evaluated from left to right. The intermediate and expression results are rounded to an integer value. Operators can act on numbers and symbols.

===== Page 21 =====
Numbers

Numbers represent data that do not vary with program delivery. Numbers can be represented in three bases: decimal (base 19), octale (base 8), hexadecimal (base 16). The binary representation is not allowed. A number will be recognized as written in a given database by the presence of a prefix or suffix (see table below). Without prefix or suffix, a number will be interpreted as a decimal number per assembler.

PREFIX BASE SUFFIX BEACH
OCTALE a Q ou O 9 à 177777
DÉCIMALE & T 0 à 65535
HEXADÉCI-
MALE $ H 9 à FFFF

Exemples : 2000 H = 8192T &5000 = $1388

===== Page 22 =====
Comment field

The comment field is the last of a source program line. It can start from the first space after the field operand. It is an optional field: it is used for program documentation and is only printed on the listing but not translated into machine language.

Switching from one field to another is done automatically by B (bar spacing), if you are not in the comment field.

In the comment field, space resumes its normal function. On the tag, operation code, operand fields, the lowercase characters are automatically replaced by the equivalent capitals. So you can type in capital letters or lower case.

The passage in column 37 activates the note "DO" to indicate to the user that the end of the line is approaching (col. 39).

Test programmes

We propose to write three test programs:

« EQUATES » «HORLOGE» et « ROUTINE »
which will serve as references in the rest of this book. To write these programs you will need to use edit commands that are "tools" to write and correct source programs. We invite you to try these "tools" by writing the test programs below.

In EDITION mode, you have a number of commands that are accessible by the keyboard control keys of your machine.

The following keys can be used:

Defalation at the top or bottom of the cursor in the text

edited.

Defaling to the right or left of the cursor on a line

published. Gia OS Inserting or erasing a character into a line of text.

IE Defaling a page down or up.

ENTERMENT Insert a new line into the text.

BE = Automatic tab or space.

Save "EQUATES"

The backup of "EQUATES" is obtained in EDITOR ORDER mode by using the command § (SAVE):

| >SO:EQUATESASM |

for backup on diskette in unit 9.

| >SC:EQUATESASM |

for backup on cassette player. The system asks you the question:

- Ready Y/N Cassette? - Yes.

Type

"EQUATES" is saved and the name of the created file is displayed on the command line.

| C: EQUATES ASM = >

For this, proceed as follows:

— Enter command V

C:EQUATES ASM

The system answers you with the question:

- Ready Y/N Cassette? - Yes.

— Rewind the tape

- Answer:

Verification Error

< descriptor > N      Are you sure Y/N ?

2.1 Editing Orders

Edit commands are active commands in EDITION mode in the program field.

Move the cursor from a position to the top Command: The J key

Effect: The command [M moves the cursor from a line to the top of the screen. If the cursor is positioned in line 9, the rest of the text visible on the screen is pushed back from a downward position, and the previous line is displayed at the top of the screen. If the @ line is the first of the source program residing in the editor, a blank line is added at the beginning of the program.

Example:

LESS SES SESESOSEL ED SEDOLE SED OD ED EDS OS OD OD & D 4
Xists:11:2:::::HORLOGY: sister rssrrstsarice

ORG $6100
INCLUD 0: EQUATES

FDB CERCL1, CERCLE, CERCL2

FDB CERCLE, TRAC3, TRASE
FDB CERCL3, CERCLE, TRAC1, TRASE

Type §
SOOO OR OOO OOO ORR ROR KCK
X::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::
ORG $6100

INCLUD 0: EQUATES

NO

FDB CERCL1, CERCLE, CERCL2
FDB CERCLE, TRAC3, TRASE

41


===== Page 41 =====
Moving the cursor down Command: The D key

Effect: The command moves the cursor from a line down the screen. The slider positioned online 23 causes a scroll from the editing area to the top of the screen and displays the following line online 23. If the cursor is positioned on the last line of the resident source program, after moving the cursor will point to a blank line that will not be added to the program text.

Example:

xDisplay the three needles

LDA #4                   ; Average increment
STA DROT
LDX #ZONSEC+8            ; Descriptor of

x seconds
JSR AFFTOR               ; Display of 1

NN a

* implementation sub-programmes

===== Page 42 =====
Move the cursor to the left Command: The BB key

Effect: The EM command moves the cursor from a character to the left of the screen. If the cursor is positioned on the @ column, an EM command will locate it at the end of the previous row. If the cursor is located online @, a move on the @ column will view a new line at the top of the screen and position the cursor in line 9 and column 39.

Example:

xXDIVISION OF CADRAN (red dots)
OBJET FCB ABSOLU, LFCUR

Type EM

XDIVISION OF THE CADRAN (red dots)
OBJET FCB ABSOLU, LFCUR

Moving the cursor from a position to the right Command: The key

Effect: The EM command moves the cursor from a character to the right of the screen. If the cursor is positioned on column 39, an M command will locate it at the beginning of the next line. If the cursor is located on line 23, a move on column 39 will view a new line at the bottom of the screen and position the cursor on line 23 and column @.

Example:

xConmutation of RAM banks
BANQ STA RLPBANC

Type @

xConmutation of RAM banks
BANQ STA RLPBANC

Control Space: The MMMM key (bar spacing)

Effect: The SSSR command (space) has several functions:

— Automatic tab in writing a source program line, except in the comment field.

— A space in the following areas:

* command line editor

* comment lines

* comment field of a source program line.

Example:

DIRECT EQU K<-8 PAGE 0
Type Pe

Type P

Type

Type

Type

Entry

Command: From keyboard, GG

Effect: The EM command, activated in the d In line 23 the text is pushed up and the cursor remains positioned at the beginning of line 23. On the command line edit the command: AU validates the previous command(s).

Example

INIT PSHS D
LDB #RESETC              ; Reset

Type EME

INIT PSHS D

LDB #RESETC              ; Reset


===== Page 44 =====
Inserting a character Command: The key

Effect: The command allows insertion of a new character from the cursor position, shifting the rest of the text to the right within the defined field. The insertion into a field of a source program line is performed until the character that was initially pointed by the cursor reaches the next field.

When the pointed character has reached the field following a new M insertion command, there is no effect.

Any character shifted beyond column 39 is permanently lost.

Example:

DEBUT LDA #DIRECT        ; Direct page
Type

DOEBUT LDA #DIRECT       ; Direct page
Type KE

DO EBUTLDA #DIRECT Direct page Type DS

DO EBUTLDA #DIRECT Direct page Type DS

DO EBUTLDA #DIRECT Direct page Type DSi

DD EBUTLDA #DIRECT Direct page Type IS

DO EBUTLDA#DIRECT Direct page Type

D EBUTLDA#DIRECT Direct page

45


===== Page 45 =====
Clearing a character Command: The M key

Effect: The MM command erases the character pointed by the cursor and moves the characters to the right of the position to the left

Pointed.

On a source program line, if the pointed field is empty to the right of the cursor, the content of the next field, having at least one character, is shifted to the left to the cursor position. If the field following the pointed field is empty in turn, it is the field +2 that is drawn to the pointed field, then the field +3,

etc.

On an empty line, the NUM command has no effect.

Example:

Type MU Type EGG Type MH Type EGG Type MS Type BEG

Type Mi Type ME

Type EGG Type KM Type KMS Type KM Type M

REPLACEMENT: Replacement of a string by another string in the resident source program

Command: Type R/<chain1 >/<chain2>/ then ENTER or R and ENTER

Effect: The command R/<chaine1>/<chaine2>/ (REPLACE) replaces, on the current line, <chaine1> with <chaine2>.

<chain1> and <chain2> can be located in any of the four program line fields or be included in other strings.

The REPLACE command used with the active ZONE command replaces all <chain1> with <chain2> in the space defined by ZONE.

<chain1 > does not differentiate between lowercase and uppercase characters. For <chain1>: <rrr> and <RRR> are equivalent chains.

<chain2> replaces <chain1> by taking into account the lowercase and uppercase characters of <chain2> in the command.

Writing in tiny characters on the command line is obtained with the keyboard in tiny function (see CAPS on)

and with the ES key. supported at the same time as desired character.

After replacing <chaine1> with <chain2> the cursor returns to its initial position.

If <chain1> and <chain2> are omitted in the command, the last strings with the R command (REPLACE) will be used. If <chain1> is not found on the current line or in the selected area, the "string Not Found" message is displayed in the comment area of the EDITOR screen.

Note that the REPLACE command does not change the amplitude of the selected area.

As for FIND, the delimiter "/" can be replaced by any other non-alphabetical character.

R/<chain1 >/<chain2>/ is equivalent to R&<chain1 >&/<chain2>&.

FIND: Search for a string in the resident source program

Command: Type F/<chaine>/ then or F then

Effect: The F/<chain>/ (FIND) command searches the first expression / < string>/ from the current position in the resident source program. <chain> can be located in any of the four program line fields.

As soon as / <chain>/ is identified, the cursor points to the first character of the string.

If / < string >/ is omitted from the command, the string specified in the previous F (FIND) command will be used for the search. The F command (FIND) can be used with the active Z command (ZONE). In this case the search extends the selected area to the line containing / < string>/.

If / < chain >/ is not found:

1. The "string not found" message is displayed in the comments area of the EDITOR screen.

2. If the search is performed with the active Z (ZONE) command, the amplitude of the zone is not changed.

FIND makes no difference between lowercases and uppercases. For FIND "ABC" and "abc" are equivalent strings. The first character after F, "/" in the example above, is a delimiter and can be replaced by any other non-alphabet character. F/<chain>/ is equivalent to F%(chain)%.

2.3 File management commands under EDITOR

File management commands can be activated in EDITOR ORDER mode. They allow the management of files containing source programs.

File commands can be a character or a character followed by a file descriptor.

If the command is a single character, the descriptor, if any, present in the left part of the command line will be used to complete the command.

The .ASM suffix and C: or 9: devices can be taken by default by the system.

The file management commands under EDITOR are:

LOAD - SAVE - MERGE - VERIFY - EOF

LOAD: Loading in the editor of a source program

Command: Type the <file descriptor > or Lt

Effect: The command L <descriptor> (LOAD) loads a source program from the device specified in the file descriptor. The load performed, the file descriptor that is taken into account by the LOAD command is displayed in the left part of the command area.

To use the LOAD command, a file descriptor is not always required:

— With the use of a SARA, this is the first file found with .ASM for suffix that will be loaded.

— If a file descriptor is present in the left part of the command line, it can be used in the LOAD command.

If you are using a SARA, the system asks the question: ‘‘Ready Y/N?' (LEP ready O/N?). If the answer is positive, the ‘Searching' (search), ‘Skip' (saute) and ‘Finding' messages are displayed. The ‘‘Skip < filename'' message is displayed on the comment line during the search phase whenever a file is skipped.

CHAPITRE 3
ASSEMBLAGE

After writing a source program it is necessary to assemble. Assemblage is an important step in the development phase of a program. The task of the assembler will be to translate the source program into machine language (object program), to construct the table of symbols used by the program, to indicate the errors encountered during assembly and to record them. The assembly can only detect:

— syntaxic errors which are errors related to the writing rules (spelling errors in language 6899 in some way). For example: LBD instead of LDB;

— errors in the validity of an operator, label, etc. For example: connection to an unknown label. An error-free program does not guarantee that the program can run correctly. In this case the logic of the programme is involved.

Before assembling, the user will have to ensure that the source program is loaded into memory, as the assembler can only process source programs residing in the publisher.

The ASSEMPTIONS allow the user to control the assembly conditions.

These options are:

IWE Wait on Errors

INL No Listing (no object listing)

ILP Line Printer (printer output)

INO No Object (no object program in memory)

INS No Symbol (no symbol table)

ISS Small Screen

Assembling OPTIONS can be combined in the ASM (assembly) EX command. Example: A/LP/WE/SS

73


===== Page 73 =====
In the absence of an option, the distribution on the screen of source and object programs is as follows:

Purpose programme, Source programme,
9 13 18 39

The source program is displayed in blue and the object program in yellow*. Error messages are viewed, in red, from the @ column, before the source line that generated the error.

The error counter is displayed in blue following the listing of source and object programs.

The symbol table (in blue) is the last on an assembly output listing. It is located between columns @ and 11.

Label/symbol, Addresses/Values,
My 6 8 1]

ASSEMBLAGE GUIDELINES are instructions for assemblers and are not translated into machine language. The assembly instructions allow:

(ii) assigning symbols and labels to numerical values. These are the EQU and SET directives;

— memory management and implementation of the object programme. These are the RMB and ORG directives;

— the definition of constants (eight and sixteen bits) and text coded in ASCII characters. These are the FCB, FDB, FCC directives;

— modification of the extended address in direct address. It's
directive SETDP ;

— act on the output of the assembly listing. These are the directives
PAGE et TITLE ;

— define the part of the programme to be assembled and the programmes to be included in the assembly phase. These are the
directives END et INCLUD.

When the assembly is completed, the INSTRUCTIONS no longer play any role.

CAUTION! The assembly command of a source program is an EDITOR command, available in COM-mode.
MANDE EDITOR.

* Blue covered in the manual.

74


===== Page 74 =====
ASSEMBLAGE: Assembly of the source program residing in the publisher

Command: Type A <file descriptor > / < Option >

or A< file descriptor > or A /<Option> or A

Effect: The command A <descriptor>/<option> (ASSEMBLAGE) is the assembly command of the source program residing in the editor. If a file descriptor is present in the assembly command, it specifies the destination (disk or cassette) and the name of the object program, otherwise the program is assembled in memory. If the left part of the editor command line contains the name of the resident source program, the file descriptor in the assembly command can be reduced to the default device: " @:" or "C:". In this case the source file name will be used with the .BIN suffix.

If in the assembly command the file descriptor is omitted, the object program will be assembled in memory, from an address that depends on whether or not the ORG assembly directive is present in the source program.

— If ORG is present, it specifies the location address.

— If ORG is not present, the object program will be installed in memory from the first free page in memory (after the symbol table).

Note that it is possible to stop the assembly listing on the screen with the STOP key. The recovery is achieved by pressing any key.

Example of memory assembly:

O: CLOCK .ASM >A

Note: For the sake of clarity the following assembly listings are presented on 89 columns.

3.1. Assembly options

/WE: Stop assembly if an error is detected

Command: Type A <file descriptor > /WE or A/WE

Effect: With each error detected, the assembly / WE option displays the message corresponding to the error and stops the current assembly. The source line containing the error is displayed. At this level the user has three possibilities to take over. By typing: — CNT/C, the system abandons assembly and returns under editor control

— C, (minus or capital) the system continues assembly but will not stop on potential errors in the continuation of the programme.

— on any touch the system resumes assembly and will stop on the next detected error.

Example: Write an error in the "HORLOGY" program, for example:

* Call from extra-monitor EXTRA Swi2

RTS

NED START

dd

[ 0:CLOCK.ASM AE

9F63 39 RTS
Bad Opcode

NED START
Missing END Statement

00002 Total Errors

/NL : Removes listing display

Command: Type A <file descriptor > /NL
ou AINL ENTREE

Effect: The assembly/NL option removes the program listing display.

e Remain only visible:

— Error messages and source lines containing errors — Error counter

— The table of symbols

Example:

0: CLOCK ASM

===== Page 92 =====
/LP: Switch printing on the printer.

Command: Type to <file descriptor >/LP or AILP

Effect: The assembly/LP option switches the printing of assembly output listings on the printer.

After printing, the end of the page is ejected and the printer is positioned at the top of the next page.

Example:

O:CLOCK.ASM > ALP

ENTER

The printer is enabled.

/NO : Removal of memory generated object program Command : Type A/NO

Effect: The /NO assembly option removes the memory generated object program. If a file descriptor and the /NO option are specified at the same time in the assembly command, the INO option will be ignored, as in any case the file descriptor directs the assembly to a device. The object program will be saved in the specified file.

Example:

O: CLOCK ASM > A/NO

The assembly is done, but the object program is not implanted in memory. This is mainly used to verify the absence of syntax error.

===== Page 93 =====
/NS : Removal of the symbol table

Command: Type A <file descriptor > /NS or AINS

Effect: The assembly option /N$ removes the table of symbols in the assembly output listing.

Example:

O: .ASM CLOCK

ENTER

The listing is displayed on the screen without the symbol table.

/SS: Editing on separate lines of source and object programs

Command: Type A <file descriptor >/SS
ou AISS ENTREE

Effect: The assembly option /$$ edits source and object programs on different lines.

Example:

O: CLOCK ASM

3.2. Assembly guidelines

Directive EQU :          ; EQUAL (assignment of a value to a symbol)
Syntax: <symbol> WHERE <expression> <comment >

Purpose: The EQU assembly directive assigns to the symbol placed in the label field the value of the expression (8 or 16 bits) placed in the field operand. These equivalences are recorded in the symbol table.

Remarks: EQU is a directive that gives the symbol a value that is not related to the program counter (PC).

The symbols defined with EQU cannot be redefined in the program suite.

The symbols used in the definition of other symbols or the calculation of an expression must be defined in advance.

To facilitate understanding of the program, it is convenient to place the EQU guidelines at the beginning of the program.

Example:

Assigning a value to a symbol.

Prog.Prog. object. source

0002 PUTC EQU $02        ; View
214E FACEXP EQU $214B    ; Exhibiting
A7E5 RLPBAN EQU $A7E5    ; Bank

Assignment of a symbol to another symbol.

—_ ~ 77 |

OOOA GETC QU OA Keyboard
OOOA POIM EQU GETC POIM=GSTC=$A

Ie ee

Assignment of a symbol to an expression.

000€ LIGN QUE POIMFPUTC LOAt+2=10C
2151 FACLO LQU FACEXP+3


===== Page 95 =====
SET Directive: Set symbol to value (temporary assignment of a value to a symbol)

Syntax: <symbol> SET <expression> <comment >

Purpose: The SET assembly directive assigns to the symbol placed in the label field the value of the expression (8 or 16 bits) placed in the operand field. These equivalences are recorded in the symbol table and identified by the S character (such as SET).

Remarks: SET is a assignment directive similar to EQU but unlike EQU, symbols defined with SET can be redefined in the remainder of the program, with a SET directive.

The symbols used in the definition of other symbols or the calculation of an expression must be defined in advance.

The SET Directive is very useful for temporarily defining reusable symbols or labels in the remainder of the programme. Note: The table of symbols indicates the last assignment of symbols defined by the SET directive.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the SET directive.

Prog. Prog object. source
4567 ETIQ SET $4567
6783 ETIQ SET $6783
0004 SYMB SBT $4
0008 SYMB SET SYMB+SYMB
678B SYMB SET SYMB+ETIQ
0000 END

00000 Total Errors

SYMB 678B S

Directive RMB :          ; Reserve Memory Bytes
memory)

Syntax: <symbol> RMB < expression> < comment >

Purpose: The RMB Assembly Directive reserves a memory area defined by the expression in the operand field. For this RMB adds the expression value to the program counter (PC).

Remarks: RMB reserves an uninitialized memory space at a particular value. The symbols used in defining or calculating the expression of the field operand must be defined before meeting the RMB directive.

With the RMB directive, the symbol in the label field is assigned to the current value of the program counter.

In the case of an assembly with a <file descriptor> no code is created in the binary file by the RMB directive. Thus, the .BIN file cannot be compared with the memory image loaded by VERIFY in the monitor.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the RMB directive.

Prog. Prog object. source
6900 ORG $6900
6900 RME 16
6910 RES1 RME 16
6920 RES2 RME 15%2 |
6940 FIN EQU t
0000 EUD
6900 ! DEBUT OBJET
16 bytes: 00000 Total Errcrs 6910 . REST . <Bytes
FIN 6940 6920 | — RES2
RES1 6910 | :
RES2 6920 20 bytes END


===== Page 97 =====
Directive ORG :          ; Origin (program counter initialization)

Syntax: ORG <expression> <comment >

Purpose: The ORG assembly directive loads the program counter (PC) at the value specified by the expression in the operating field. The following instructions are assembled by incrementing PC. Thus ORG defines the beginning of the object program that results from the assembly.

Remarks: There may be several ORGs in a source program.

The ORG Directive should not be written with a label.

If ORG is omitted from the source program, the program counter is loaded to:

— 0000 if assembly is requested with a destination file. Note: A program assembled in 0000 does not load on MO6 (unless an offset is specified).

— the value of the first page following the symbol table, if the assembly is requested in memory.

A memory page corresponds to the 256 bytes defined between two consecutive values of strong weight of address. For example:

[A Page nn ia
0000 | 0100 | nnQ0 {|

The end of available memory label (ENDMEM) can be used in the expression to calculate the beginning of the object program.

Example: The sequences below show the consequences of the presence or absence of the ORG directive in the source program (‘‘Rutine').

A- ORG is present and the expression uses ENDMEM.

0:ROUTINE.ASM
ENTER
XX IO IK A KK AS OUS— PROGRAMMES * X k KR CIO AK K

Directive FCB :          ; Form Constant Byte (definition of a constant)
one byte)

Syntax: <symbol> FCB <expr>, <expr>, <expr> <commentary >

Purpose: The FCB Assembly Directive stores 8-bit constants in memory positions defined by the current program counter (PC) value. For each byte loaded, the PC is incremented by 1.

Remarks: FCB creates an initialized memory space at a particular value.

FCB can set several constants separated by a comma.

Constants can be numerical values, characters, symbols, expressions.

The constants greater than eight bits will be truncated at eight bits and reported by the "Operation Too Large" message.

The symbols used in the definition or calculation of the expression contained in the field operand can be defined after meeting the FCB directive.

With the FCB directive, the symbol in the label field is assigned to the current value of the program counter.

The constants are returned on the object program listing to: four bytes per line.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the FCB directive.

Prog. Prog object. source
5000 ORG $5000
5000 04 08 41 ZE DON FCB $04,8T,'A,$22+12T
Operator Too Large
5004 FF O1 DATA FCB 255,257
5006 56 THOM FCB MO6
0056 MOG EQU 86T
0000 END

100


===== Page 100 =====
Directive FDB :          ; Form Double Byte Constant (definition of a cons-
bytes or a word)

Syntax: <symbol> FDB <expr>, <expr>, <expr> <commentaire >

Purpose: The FDB Assembly Directive stores 16-bit constants in memory positions defined by the current program counter (PC) value. For each word loaded, the PC is incremented by 2.

Remarks: FDB creates an initialized memory space at a particular value.

FDB can set several constants separated by a comma.

Constants can be numerical values, characters, symbols, expressions.

Constants greater than sixteen bits will be truncated at sixteen bits. Symbols used in the definition or calculation of the expression contained in the field operand can be defined after meeting the FDB directive.

With the FDB directive, the symbol in the label field is assigned to the current value of the program counter.

Constants are returned to the object program listing at two words per line.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the FDB directive.

Prog. Prog object. source
4678 ORG $4678
4678 5678 OOOA MOT FDR £5678.8T.'W. S277At1234T

467C 0057 2C4ac

4680 O1 03 MOTA FCB 65537,65539
Operator Too Large
4682 C2 THOM bo MCE

0o7c2 MO6 LQuU 1966T

0000 END

10)


===== Page 101 =====
Directive FCC :          ; Form Constant Character String
constant string)

Syntax: <symbol> FCC < deli> < chain > < deli> < comment >

Purpose: The FCC Assembly Directive stores strings in memory positions defined by the current program counter (PC) value. With each character the PC is incremented by 1.

Remarks: FCC creates an initialized memory space at a particular value.

FCC defines the string to store between two identical delimiters.

These delimiters can be any of the printable characters.

The space used in the operand field causes automatic tabulation. For this reason it is preferable to use CNT/S (inverted video space) to generate a space in a string.

With the FCC directive, the symbol in the label field is assigned to the current value of the program counter.

The Strings are returned to the object program listing at a rate of four characters per line.

Note that FCC /A/ is equivalent to FCC'A

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the FCC directive.

Prog.Prog. object. source
6600 ORG $6600
6600 54 52 4F SO | TROP FCC ! TROP GRAND!
6604 20 47 52 41
6608 4 44

102


===== Page 102 =====
Directive SETDP :        ; Set Direct Page
(direct page positioning)

Syntax: SETDP < expression> < comment >

Purpose: The SETDP Assembly Directive defines a "page @" in memory space, where extended address will be changed to direct address. This will speed up the execution speed and decrease the length of the object program (two or three bytes instead of three or four bytes).

Remarks: SETDP does not admit label.

The SETDP directive does not change the microprocessor 6899's page 9 (DP) registry.

The user will have to plan for loading in accordance with the last value of the "page @". Failure to load the register (DP) would lead to an error in the execution of the program but not to assembly. The system cannot know the intentions of the programmer !! The value of the page register (PNR) is called "base page".

At initialization of the system, "page 9" is positioned at 99.

The SETDP directive can be used several times in a program sequence.

The operating field of a SETDP directive can be occupied by: a constant, a symbol, an expression.

The symbols used in the definition of SETDP shall be defined upstream.

If the calculation of SETDP expression leads to a value greater than eight bits, the "page 9" will be loaded with the eight bits of low weight

and the message "Operation Too Large" will be generated by the assembly.

Note that the user always has the option of subtracting a direct address instruction from "page 9" by forcing the assembler to use the address extended by (>).

Example: The following sequences show the various employment opportunities of the SETDP Directive.

104


===== Page 104 =====
PAGE Directive: Page Jump Syntax: PAGE

Purpose: The PAGE Assembly Directive moves the printer paper forward at the beginning of the next page.

If printing is not requested (by /LP) the PAGE directive is ineffective.

Note: Inserting white lines into the listing is done simply by putting white lines into the source program.

TITLE Directive: Title (definition of a title) Syntax: TITLE < string >

Purpose: The TITLE Assembly directive specifies a page header by the string contained in the field operand. TITLE is active with the assembly/LP option. It has no effect in other cases.

Remarks: The string is delimited by the end of the line and may contain 26 characters. This number can be increased to 32 if the programmer uses the rest of the operation code field.

The specified string is printed at the top of each page until another TITLE directive has been encountered.

TITLE does not admit a symbol in the label field, nor comment.

Example:

TITLE THIS IS A TITLE*26carxx

TITLE this is still a TITLE+32car+t
END

105


===== Page 105 =====
INCLUD Directive: Inclusion of a source program Syntax: INCLUD <descriptor> <comment >

Purpose: The INCLUD assembly directive merges, at assembly, the file specified in the field operating with the source program resident in memory. This is the equivalent of an M (MERGE) command that only occurs at the time of assembly.

Remarks: The INCLUD directive is not usable with a file residing on the cassette player for the following reasons:

— Multipass assembly requires several passages on the source program. It would therefore be necessary to reposition the reader several times.

— With a cassette player, it is not possible, at the same time, to read the source program and save the object program.

INCLUD does not admit embedded inclusion: the program to be included should therefore not contain the INCLUD directive.

If INCLUD is encountered during inclusion, the error message "BAD INCLUD" (inclusion error) is generated. It is the same if an attempt to include is made from SARA.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the INCLUD directive.

Prog. Prog object. source
LSPS SSS SESS PS SOSESSESSS® SS OS DARES SESS ES 4
Xi:::::::::1:: HORLOGE: ::::::::::3111::1!1::!

* Ignition or extinction of a point

106


===== Page 106 =====
Directive END :          ; End of source programme
Syntax: END <expression> <comment >

Purpose: The END assembly directive marks the limit of the source program. The program lines behind END will be ignored by the assembler.

Remarks: If the END assembly directive is omitted at the end of a program, the system will generate the error message: "Missing END statement" (the END directive is missing).

END does not admit a symbol in the label field.

The optional expression in the operand field specifies the program execution address. This value will be used to specify the run address in the file when creating the object program.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the END directive.

* MAIN PROGRAMME

DEBUT LDA #DIRECT        ; Direct page
TFR A, DP
JSR INITIALISATION
LDY #HORLO               ; Pointer on the

TN

* Call from extra-monitor EXTRA Swie

RTS

END START

107


===== Page 107 =====
CALL Directive: Calling a monitor sub-program Syntax: < symbol > CALL < expression > < comment >

Purpose: The CALL assembly directive allows you to call a system monitor routine. The byte in the operand field specifies the code of the entry point.

Remarks: CALL generates the machine code corresponding to a SWI followed by the byte contained in the operand field (see Annex A, ‘access to monitor routines'). The operating field can be defined by an expression. If the calculation of the expression leads to a value greater than 8 bits, it will be truncated at 8 bits and reported by the ‘Operand Too Large' message.

As no test is performed on the operand, if the value of the expression does not correspond to an input point of the monitor, a malfunction can occur and thus modify internal registers of the system.

The symbols used in the expression can be defined after the meeting of the CALL Directive. Breakpoints are allowed on CALL and the execution of the programme can be taken over by a CONTINUE. CALL is the equivalent of a JSR.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the CALL directive.

Note: The call to an extra-monitor sub-program is made by SWI2. The routine number then passed through

Register B of 6809. Stop points are not allowed on the SW!2.

6745 ORG $6700+545
xMonitor calls 6745 3F OA CLAV CALL SOA Keyboard 6747 3F 04 COL CALL color 6749 3F 08 SINCE CALL GETL-PLOT Bip *Extra monitor calls
674B C6 18 LDB #318      ; Circle
674D 103F SwI2
0000 END

108


===== Page 108 =====
GOTO directive: Jumping to a monitor program Syntax: <symbol> GOTO <expression> <commercial >

Purpose: The GOTO assembly directive allows you to refer the main program to the system monitor. The byte contained in the field operand specifies the entry point.

Remarks: GOTO generates the machine code corresponding to a SWI followed by the byte contained in the operating field and forces bit 7 to one (see Appendix A ‘‘access to monitor routines').

The operating field can be defined by an expression. If the calculation of the expression leads to a value greater than 8 bits, it will be truncated at 8 bits and reported by the ‘'Opernde Too Large' message. No tests are performed on the operand. If the value of the expression does not correspond to a monitor input point, a malfunction may occur and the internal system records may be modified. Symbols used in the expression can be defined after meeting the GOTO directive. GOTO is the equivalent of a JMP. As the monitor sub-programs have been completed by an RTS, when this instruction is met, the PC will point to the instruction following the last call for a sub-program. Stop points are allowed on a GOTO. The implementation of the programme may be resumed by a CONTINUE.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the GOTO directive.

6745 ORG $6700+845
xMonitor jumps 6745 3F 8A CLAV GOTO SOA Keyboard 6747 3F 8A CLAV1 GOTO SBA keyboard 6749 3F 84 COUL GOTO $04 Color 674B 3F 83 SON GOTO GETL-PLOT Bip
0000 END

109


===== Page 109 =====
STOP Directive: Back to status ‘‘monitor control wait'? Syntax: < symbol > STOP < > < comment >

Purpose: The STOP Assembly Directive stops program execution and gives back hand to the monitor who is positioned in the ‘`manual waiting''' #.

Remarks: STOP generates in the object program the BD B000 instruction (JSR $B000) which has the effect of handing the monitor. STOP does not admit expression in the field operand. The STOP Assembly Directive does not allow a stop point. This would also make no sense because a STOP already generates a stop point itself. Note that STOP and END should not be confused.

STOP is the monitor-controlled return directive while END defines the part of the source program to be assembled.

Example: The sequence below shows the use of the STOP directive.

4FBD 16 FCC4 LBRA SPINT To loop x operation 4FCO BD BOOO STOP Back to monitor

110


===== Page 110 =====
Where is the object program located? What is the end of the source program?

These are the questions many programmers ask themselves at the beginning of the use of a new assembler.

Before answering these questions, it is necessary to recall the location of the user memory in the addressable field.

Page 0 2000
monitor 9100 Page 0 extramonitor 2300 Fixed memory SFFF 6000 Bank Bank Bank Bank Bank Bank Bank Bank Bank
1 2 3 4 5 6
OFFF

It is in these memory zones that must be housed:

- The source program

- The symbol table

- The object programme

The source program position is selected by the system.

It will be at the beginning of the user space.

The source program will be followed by the symbol table.

The rest of the memory, which we will call "free memory", may be occupied by the object program.

The position of the object program in "free memory" is defined by the Org (original) assembly directive.

Without instructions to assemble ORG in the source program, the object program will be resident from the first page following the symbol table.

A special ENDMEM tag defines the end of RAM memory.

111


===== Page 111 =====
* The M06 has an addressable user RAM of $2300 to SOFFF. The $6000 to $9FFF zone is organized into switchable banks.

2300

Source programme

Table of symbols

Programme

ENDMEM OFFF

ENDMEM can be used to define a position in memory.

Example: orc ENDMEM- $3000 ... is acceptable by the assembler.

Note: "*" or "." are special symbols that represent the current value of the program counter. These symbols may be used in calculating expressions.

Example: DIRECT EQU */256 (whole division)

An attempt to implant the object program (by ORG) in the area occupied by the source program is not allowed and generates the message: "Bad memory" (bad memory).

Therefore, it is not possible to overwrite the source program with the object program.

A source program may be long enough and may not leave enough room in memory to implement the object program, or may require switching a bank.

112


===== Page 112 =====
There are two possible solutions to this problem:

1 - Request object program assembly on a file. In this case all available memory is used by the source program.

2 - Save the source program to a file and call only in the assembly phase by INCLUD directive.

For example:

DEBUT ORG *
INCLUD PROGSOU (source)
END START

In this case all (or almost all) memory is available to implement the object program.

The table below summarizes the implementation of the object program according to the input parameters.

The Org Assembly Directive is present in the source programme.

Command Request assembly with a file descriptor.

A <descriptor>

Result The object program is installed on the assigned device. ORG specifies the original address of the object program.

Command Request assembly without file descriptor. A file is present in the left side.

" descriptor " AC

Result The object program is installed on the device of the current file. ORG specifies the original address of the object program.

113


===== Page 113 =====

Result

The object program is located in memory at the address specified by ORG.

The Org assembly directive is missing in the source program.

Command

Result

The object program is installed on the assigned device with address 6000 as its origin. *

The object program is installed in memory from the first page that follows the symbol table.

115


===== Page 115 =====
CHAPITRE 4
LE MONITEUR

The monitor is part of the program residing in the memory of the ROM cartridge.

The monitor is both a tool for checking and modifying the contents of the memory and internal records of the microprocessor 6899, and a tool for executing and developing the object programs from the assembler.

The monitor offers the possibility to run a program in whole or in part and to know the intermediate values of the 6899 registers and the contents of the memoirs for possible modification.

The monitor uses the entire screen and permanently displays the names of the microprocessor records on line 9.

PC A B DP CC X Y U S

#

Line 25 (red) is intended to receive comments and error messages from the monitor.

Example:

- Ready Y/N Cassette?

(list ready O/N?)

Monitor inputs and outputs appear on the screen:

— Blue entries on black background

— Yellow outings on black background (blue covered in manual)

The "Monitor Control Expectation" status is expressed by "#" to the left of the cursor.

Access to the monitor

To access the monitor, the user has two entries: — From MENU. — From the Editor.

117


===== Page 116 =====
From MENU:

The command of MENU EI activates the monitor block. Without file descriptor:

MONITOR |. |

The monitor screen is generated and positioned in the monitor control waiting state.

PC A B DP CC X Y U S
#

With file descriptor:
MONITORS 0: CLOCK.BIN

ou MONITOR | HORLOGE.BIN |

ou MONITOR | HORLOGE |

ou MONITOR | C:HORLOGE |

Note: 9: C: and .BIN are optional and are taken by default. The monitor screen is generated as above.

The object file specified in the command is loaded in memory to addresses that were defined at the time of saving the object program.

From PEDITOR:

The command in EDITOR ORDER mode allows access to the monitor (with possible loading of an object file).

D:HORLOGY ASM > X<file>

The monitor screen is generated as above. The transition from editor to monitor does not change the contents of the memory. Only object files can be loaded by the monitor. A source file call under monitor causes the error: "File format error".

118


===== Page 117 =====
4.1 Monitor controls

Monitor controls are active controls from the keyboard, the monitor being in "manual waiting" status.

INPUT: entry

Command: The monitor in command waiting status "#"

Type: ID or: IH

Effect: The command (H or D) (INPUT) declares the digital mode of input into the monitor.

The codes allowed are:

DÉCIMAL : D

HEXADÉCIMAL : H

The undeclared code must be specified in the command by its prefix or suffix.

A new declaration cancels the previous declaration.

If the entry declaration is omitted, the MONITOR will interpret the default hexadecimal entries.

The input values are displayed in blue on the screen.

Example:

#ID (current input code: decimal)

#8000 (8000 en decimal)
#8000H or #88000 (8000 hexadecimal)

#1H

(current input code: hexadecimal)

#8000 (8000 hexadecimal)
#48000 ou #8000T {8009 en decimal)

119


===== Page 118 =====
( ADDRESS): Display and/or change the contents of a memory address or internal registers of the microprocessor 6899

Command: The monitor in "waiting" status "#" Type: <expression> / <content> <expression> [or

Effect: The command (ADRESSE) / allows to display and/or modify the contents of the memory box whose address is specified in the command.

If the address is omitted, the monitor will point to the last specified address.

If no address has been specified previously, the monitor will point to address 9909.

The address can be defined by a label contained in the symbol table from the last assembly, or by the symbols representing the internal records of 6899 (PC, A, B, D, DP, CC, X, Y, U, S).

The number of bytes examined from the pointed address depends on the mode of output.

In mnemonic mode, the number of bytes examined may be one to five. In numerical mode the number of bytes depends on the value of the N command parameter (see below). The character "=" only visualizes the content of the last pointed address.

After the "/" character that indicates the end of the address, the monitor displays the contents of the memory or registry.

Note that a @ precedes all hexadecimal numbers beginning with an alphabetical character, to avoid confusion between hexadecimal numbers and microprocessor records. After the display of the memory content, the system is awaiting the entry of a new value to replace the previous one. This entry is optional.

The key allows you to look at the following bytes without returning to the point "command waiting".

The key allows to examine the previous bytes, but by decrement only one byte.

The key completes the command and returns to the command waiting point.

===== Page 119 =====

PULS CC,A,E,X,U

Note: If the input and output modes are not defined, the monitor forces the input in hexadecimal and the output in mnemo- mode.

MNEMONIC MODE

Command: The monitor in command waiting status "#" Type: M

Effect: The M command (MNEMONIC MODE) allows you to reconstitute at the exit of the monitor the instructions in symbolic language of the source program. This operation is also called "disassembly".

For this, the monitor examines the memory in packets from one to five bytes following the instruction.

The address of the order must point to the first byte of an instruction, otherwise the disassembly of the object program would make no sense.

If the monitor cannot interpret the content of a byte, it will display a question mark "?"

If no monitor output mode is requested, the system will force the mnemonic mode.

INDIRECTION

Command: Type: after an address value whose content you want to know.

Effect: The command (INDIRECTION) allows to introduce a level of indirection in the examination of the contents of the memoirs.

The INDIRECTION command uses the current output content to send the new memory.

If the output is in digital mode on a byte or in ASCII mode, the monitor will complete the address with the next byte.

When output on two bytes, the value represents the address to be examined. In mnemonic mode, the monitor will point to the address indicated in the current line. If no address is present or the instruction does not touch the PC, the INDIRECTION command is ineffective and repeats the current line.

This command is very useful for examining the destination of connections, for example: JSR, JMP, BRA, etc.

Note: If the instruction touches the PC, the command visualizes the contents of the PC address after modification by this instruction.

Examples: #N2 (digital mode 2 bytes)

COMPUTER MODE

Command: The monitor in command waiting status "#" Type: <expression> =

To the right of the sign " =", the result of the calculation of the expression will be displayed.

Effect: The MONITOR CALCULATOR MODE allows you to evaluate an expression and display the result.

All expressions accepted by the assembler are evaluated by the monitor.

The labels in the expression to be evaluated must also be present in the table of symbols from the last assembly.

The evaluation of an expression can be used to examine the corresponding memory.

Negative results are expressed as complementary plus 1. This way of representing numbers is called "addition to 2"

For example:

131


===== Page 130 =====
GO: Run

Command: The monitor in command waiting status "#" Type: G <expression>

Effect: The command G <expression> (GO) allows you to run a program from the address defined by the command expression.

The GO monitor command is equivalent to an address jump (JMP instruction).

The labels used in the expression evaluation must be present in the symbol table from the last assembly. If running the program the system encounters a software interruption (SWI), it will display the message:

"8 BRK (address)" and will stop implementing the program.

This message (index 8) indicates that a breakpoint has been positioned by the user in his program. Under the supervision of the monitor, only eight points can be placed numbered from 9 to 7. SWI can be used to return to the end of running the user program, under monitor control.

Example: #GDEBUT (Program Delivery "HORLOGY")

132


===== Page 131 =====
BREAK: stop

Command: Monitor in command waiting status "#" Type: K <expression >

Effect: The K <expression> command (BREAK) positions a point to the address specified by the command expression. The user has the possibility to position eight stop points numbered from 0 to 7 in the order of programming.

Point number 8 is displayed by the monitor when it encounters the software interruption (SWI) in the user program. The positioning address of the stopping points may be defined by a constant, label or expression.

The labels used must be present in the symbol table from the last assembly.

A return under the control of the editor or MENU erases previously scheduled breakpoints.

A stop point that is not positioned on the first byte of an instruction will destroy a byte of this instruction and cause unpredictable effects on execution!!

YOU MUST NEVER DO THIS!!!

The technique of breakpoints is to insert a SWI software interruption on each address pointed by a breakpoint.

Therefore, a stop point cannot be programmed in the SYSTEM, which is in ROM memory.

Example: #K4F64 ENTER #GDEBUTE

O BRK @ 4F64

133

CONTINUED: continue

Command: The monitor in "waiting for command" "#" condition after displaying a stop point. Type: C [n]

Effect: The command C {n] (CONTINUED) allows you to continue running a program suspended by a breakpoint.

[n] indicates the number of passages through the stop point that must be done in order for the program to be suspended. At each stop point is associated a ‘‘number of passages'? which is initialized to 1 by instruction K.

If parameter n is omitted, 1 is taken by default and the program will run until the next stop point defined by the user or monitor.

If the last breakpoint has been positioned on a connection to a program area in ROM, the MONITOR will display the message "Cans Continue" (cannot continue) (see BREAK command).

Note: the G (GO) command gives the number of passes associated with BREAKPOINTS to −.

Example: #KZER ENTER #GDEBUTE
O BRK @ ZER
#C ENTER

O BRK @ ZER

MOVE: moving a block of bytes

Command: The monitor in condition ‘‘command waits' ‘‘#的 Type: J <adr. source> <adr. destination bytes > PRE

Effect: The J command (MOVE) transfers a block of bytes from the "source" address to the "destination" address. <n. Bytes> represents the length of the block to be moved.

If the ‘source' address is greater than the ‘destination' address, the transfer will be realized by incrementing the ‘source' address to ‘source' + n.

If the address ‘‘destination' is not greater than the address ‘'source', the transfer will be carried out by decrementing the address ‘‘source' to ‘‘source'. This way of achieving a MOVE is necessary to allow the possibility of overlap of source and destination areas.

An attempt to transfer to a ROM memory area is, of course, ineffective and is not reported by a system message. Since the MOVE command does not test the ‘‘destination' address, care must be taken not to overwrite a useful RAM area. Example: source program, symbol table, page 00, etc...

The MOVE command is a command to handle with infinite precautions.

Example:

#D5000 5007

5000 FF 00 FF 00 FF OO FF 00

#D4F62 4F69

4F62 86 61 1F 8B BD 4F 54 10 .a...OT.

#J4F62 5000 8

5000 86 61 1F 8B BD 4F 54 10 .a...OT.

PRINT: Print

Command: The monitor in command waiting status "#" Type: P

Effect: The P command (PRINT) allows you to switch the MONITOR display on the printer.

After receiving the PRINT command, the monitor requests confirmation of the command by sending the message "Printer Ready
Y/N? ».

If the answer is positive, the PRINT command is executed. If the answer is negative, the PRINT command is cancelled and the monitor is waiting for a new command.

Switching the display on the printer preserves the screen contents.

During the display period on the printer, echoes of commands from the keyboard are not generated. The rows are displayed as a block after validation by the ENTER key.

A second PRINT command generates a jump to the top of the next page on the printer and returns the display to the screen by deleting the previous content.

Example: #P的 Printer Ready Y/N?

(the display is switched to the printer)

Gi

(The printer performs a page jump, the screen is erased and the display is returned to the screen)

SAVE: Saving an object program

Command: The monitor in command waiting status "#" Type: S<descriptor> <adr.1> <adr.2> <adr.3>

Effect: The command § <file descriptor> <address1> <address2 > <address3 > (SAVE) saves an object program or data on the device assigned in the command.

If the file descriptor does not contain a suffix, the monitor will force the default .BIN suffix.

Device designation is not required in the command. The floppy drive @ or SARA will be used by default.

The memory area to be saved is between <address1> and <address2>, <address1> specifying the beginning and <address2> the end of the memory area to be saved.

< address3> is the address that will be used to start running the program.

< addresses 1, 2 and 3> may be specified by a constant, label or expression, provided that the labels used are present in the table of symbols from the last assembly.

If you use a SARA, the MONITOR will ask the question: " Cassette Ready Y/N?". If the answer is positive the program will be saved.

Example:

#S0: HORLOGE.OBJ 4F50 4FC2 4F62

(HORLOGY1.OBJ is saved.) The same command can also write:

#SHORLOGE BANQ 4FC2 DEBUT

(HORLOGE.BIN is saved on diskette, if present, if not on cassette)

===== Page 143 =====
LOAD: Loading an object program

Command: The monitor in command waiting status "#" Type: L<descriptor> [<offset >]

Effect: The command L <file descriptor> <offset> (LOAD) loads in memory an object program that has been saved on the device assigned in the command.

<offset> is optional in the LOAD command and allows translation of the object program.

The monitor loads the program counter (PC) with the run address that was specified in the program backup.

If (offset) is present in the command, the program counter (PC) will be loaded to the value obtained by adding the offset to the execution address.

Note: The program execution address can be set in the monitor by <adr3> in the SAVE command, or in the editor, when the object program is created by the assembler; The execution address is then defined by the presence of the label in the operating field of the END directive, which addresses the first instruction to be executed in the program.

If no label or symbol is specified with the END directive, the assembler will load 8600 as the execution address.

Example:
#L0O: HORLOGE. OBJ

HORLOGY.OBJ is loaded to the address specified when saving
garde. (4F50)

#LO: HORLOGE 0040

HORLOGE.BIN is loaded at the address specified when backup plus #49 (4F90).

VERIFY: verification

Command: The monitor in command waiting status "#" Type: V<descriptor> <adr.1> <adr.2> <adr.3>

Effect: Command V <file descriptor> <address1> <address2 >

< address3 > (VERIFY) com-

parse the contents of the file defined by the descriptor with the memory area between <address1> and <address2>.

<address1 > beginning of zone

<address2> end of zone

< address3> execution address

If the contents are the same, the cursor will reposition and wait for a new monitor command.

If the specified memory area and the designated file are different, a "Verification error" error message is displayed on the monitor's comment line.

CAUTION: adr1, adr2, adr3 must be EXACTLY the same as those used in SAVE or the beginning, end and execution address of a file created by the assembler. In addition, the program assembled to a file may not match the memory image if it does not contain a CONTINU code set. For example, successive ORGs can create a segmented file; Similarly, RMBs increment the program counter without creating code. In this

case, memory image is not comparable to the object program file.

CHAPITRE 5
FILE MANAGEMENT

FILE MANAGEMENT allows the use of files on diskettes and cassettes.

It consists of seven routines accessible by the MENU that allow:

— draw up the catalogue of a diskette (DIRECTORY)

— copying a file (COPY)

— rename a file (RENAME)

— delete a file (KILL)

— formatting a diskette (FORMAT)

— to return to the top page

DIRECTORY : catalogue The command applies only to diskettes. The command of MENU EI activates the DIRECTORY line

E DIRECTORY

The system awaits a disk drive number between ÿ and 3 and then validation by the EE key.

If no player number is specified before the EE command, the system uses the default @ drive.

A number that is not included in the range of 4 causes the "Bad parameter" message to be displayed (bad parameter) and the return under MENU control.

A number corresponding to an absent player causes the display of the "Disk Not Ready" message and the return under MENU control.

DIRECTORY (catalogue) displays the list of programs saved in the diskette positioned in the specified drive.

Line 25 (red) is intended to receive comments and error messages under the control of the DIRECTORY routine.

Example: Disk not Ready or Press any Key disc unready or press a key to continue)

===== Page 148 =====

1. File name (maximum eight characters)

2. Suffixe (default). ASM Source Programme

.BIN Object Program Note: For file name and suffix, the user can choose other names. These names may contain all printable characters (not highlighted) except " < > " ? " " and "i"

3. File type. .A Programme assembler (source) .M Programme machine (object)

4. Data type. .A ASCII .B Biennial

5. Number of blocks used by the program on the diskette. (size of a block = 1 K byte in single density). 2 K byte in double density

The return under the control of MENU is achieved by pressing any key.

Note: The DIRECTORY consultation does not erase source and object programs in memory. So you can use this function at any time to control the records and filename changes you have just made. DIRECTORY also lets you know if a diskette is initialized. With a floppy disk that is not formatted, the system returns the message: Unreadable Disk.

The DIRECTORY command also allows you to know the place still available on the diskette by doing:

Max diskette capacity minus sum of blocks used taking into account 2 blocks required for diskette catalogue.

COPY: copy between diskettes or between cassette and diskette The command of MENUEgactive the COPY line:

Æ copy

COPY awaits the first file descriptor that must be the file to copy.

The entry area is limited to fourteen characters and validated by the EU command.

Line 25 (red) is intended to receive error messages.

ge copy. To

The second area, also limited to fourteen characters, is occupied by the destination file and validated by EEA.

Its task performed, COPY returns directly under the control of MENU.

COPY allows full copying of a file by optionally changing its name.

With two floppy drives, COPY can copy a file from one drive to another by specifying the numbers of the devices used in the corresponding file descriptors.

With a single disk drive, COPY can copy a file to the same diskette by changing the file name. If the device is not specified in the source descriptor, the system will use the default @ drive. Authorized reader numbers are:
1, 2 et 3.

The suffix is indispensable in the source file descriptor. The system will complement the recipient descriptor with the source file elements.

COPY also allows copies of the cassette to a diskette and vice versa.

RENAME: Name change

The command applies only to diskettes. The MENUEI command activates the RENAME line:

RENAME | | AS

RENAME awaits the first file descriptor that must be the file 4 rename.

The input area is limited to fourteen characters and validated by the ENTER command.

Line 25 (red) is intended to receive error messages.

RENAME | _| AS | | |

The second area, also limited to fourteen characters, is occupied by the new file descriptor and validated by GE.

The name change made, the system returns under the control of MENU.

The result check can be obtained by ordering
DIRECTORY.

RENAME allows the name change of a file on a diskette only.

The first file descriptor contains the name of the file to be renamed, the second the new file name.

If the new file name corresponds to a file already existing in the diskette, the "File already exists" message is displayed.

If the device is not specified in the descriptor to be renamed, the system will use the default @ drive. If the device is omitted in the new name, the system will use the file descriptor to rename. Authorized reader numbers are 4, 1, 2 and 3. The suffix is essential in the file descriptor to rename.

Example:

RENAME: CLOCKASM
(sum 9 : HORLOGY.ASM in 8 : HORLOGY!.ASM)

RENAMES WATCH1.ASM

(sum @ : HORLOGY1.ASM in 9 : HORLOGY1.ESS)

KILL: deletion of a file

The command applies only to diskettes. The MENU command Activates the KILL line:

GH KILL | |

KILL expects the file descriptor to be destroyed.

The input area is limited to fourteen characters and validated by the command

Line 25 (red) is intended to receive error messages or questions to the user. After validation of the entry, the KILL command sends the "Are you sure Y/N?" message to the comment line (Are you sure Y/N?).

If the answer is positive, the file is destroyed, and the system returns under MENU control.

The KILL command erases the file defined by the file descriptor, removes its references in the catalog and releases the space it occupies on the diskette.

If the device is not specified in the file descriptor to be destroyed, the system will use the default @ drive.

The suffix is required in the file descriptor to be destroyed. The KILL command only works on a file residing on a diskette.

Example:

(Let's create the file "DETRUIT.FIC" so we can destroy it in the rest of our example)

EX COPY

(®@: DETRUIT.FIC is created on the diskette of drive 9)

EH, KILL-DETRUTFIC

The system responds

- Yes. Are you sure Y/N? &(9 : DETRUIT.FIC is destroyed)

FORMAT: initialization of a diskette

The command applies only to diskettes. The MENU command activates the FORMAT line.

FORMAT | |

FORMAT awaits the entry of a diskette player number between @ and 4.

This entry is mandatory: unlike the other commands, the @-doctor is not taken by default.

validates the specified drive.

Line 25 (red) is intended to receive error messages or questions to the user. After validation of the input, the FORMAT command sends the "Are you sure Y/N?" message to the comment line (are you Sir O/N?).

If the answer is positive the diskette placed in the specified drive is initialized (also called formatted) and the system returns under MENU control.

The diskette shall not be protected, i.e. its protective notch shall be free.

After the initialization phase, the diskette catalogue is empty. The initialization of a diskette erases all the data it could previously contain.

Any new diskette must be initialized before being used.

Example:

(we suggest you unstart the new diskette that you placed in the @ drive)

FORMAT no

ENTER

The system responds

Are you sure Y/N?

fa(The @ drive turns. In about three minutes your diskette will be initialized or formatted.)

PRINTER COLUMNS: declaration of the number of columns of the printer

The MENU command will enable the PRINTER COLUMNS line.

El PRINTER COLUMNS 1=492=80 | |

PRINTER COLUMNS expects one of two digits representing the number of columns of the printer.

1: 49 columns (type PR99-949) 2: 89 columns (type PR9Y-989 or PR90-600)

Line 25 (red) is intended to receive error messages. After entering one of the two digits, the system returns under MENU control. A different character of 1 or 2 causes the error Bad Parameter(s) (bad parameter). By default, the system forces the option 89 columns. The command call, with or without changing the number of columns, returns the counter line to @, i.e. the assembler cartridge considers that the printer is positioned at the top of a page. Note that it is possible to modify, under monitor control, the registers commanding the printer settings. The default values are: — The number of lines of text per page:

64 lines LINCNT 1 byte — The total size of a page:

72 lines PAGLEN 1! byte

PAGLEN > LINCNT
These records are in RAM and can be modified manually from the MONITOR. Address in $0021-$@022 point on the LANCNT register. PAGLEN is the byte following LINCNT. To change these parameters under monitor:

#12

Format of an object file: on a cassette or diskette.

1. File type in the input catalog is equal to 2.

2. The file consists of a series of data records followed by a record of the execution address.

3. The data recording format is as follows:

Decalation Length Description (byte) (byte) 0 ] type of recording = @ 1 2 length of recording = N 3 2 loading address 5 N data

4. The recording format of the execution address is as follows:

Decalation Length Description (byte) (byte) 0 1 recording type = $FF 1 2 recording length = 0000 3 2 execution address Warning

RESET should be used only in cases of absolute necessity. System integrity is not guaranteed after the use of RESET. It is advisable not to use RESET with a diskette in place in a drive, as in some cases the system may attempt writing on the diskette present. Many hours of work can thus be reduced to nothing!!

APPENDIX A
MICROPROCESSEUR 6809
INSTRUCTIONS AND ADDRESSING

Extract from Microprocessors and peripherals, edited by Thomson Semiconductors, 45, avenue de l'Europe, 78140 Velizy, telephone
(1) 39.46.97.19, télex 698 866 F .

The 6809 circuit is an 8-bit microprocessor of revolutionary design, using modern programming techniques such as normalization of implantation in memory, redirection and modular programming.

This 3rd generation contribution to the 6800 family offers architectural improvements that include additional registers, instructions and address modes.

The basic instructions of any computer are particularly improved by the presence of powerful address modes. The available address mode set of 6809 is currently the most complete of existing microprocessors. The software and hardware features of the circuit make it an ideal processor for running programs in advanced languages or for standard applications.

e 10 address modes Upward compatibility of address modes with the 6800 family. Direct address in all memory space. Long relative connections. Relative program counter. Indirect. Extended indexed address. constant movements 0, 5, 8, 16 bits accumulator displacements 8, 16 bits self-incrementation/decrementation e Improved stack handling e 1464 instructions

PROGRAMMABLE MICROPROCESSOR REGISTERS

15 0
