M06 Ext.

5.11 Copy of a file # input points: COPYO (12), COPY1 (13) # input point: NEW-COPY (72) #* input parameters

- register 6809 X Point source file. - register 6809 Y Point destination file,

The X and Y records point to an area of 20 bytes. bytes 0-7 Name

Bytes 8-10 Extension bytes 11-18 Comment byte 19 Drive number.

- DEBZON (216BH-216CH) Working memory area - FINZON (216EH-216FH) End of this area

#* return parameters - SWPFLG (219DH) Flag: should we swap?

The minimum size of the buffer is 20 bytes. But a fairly large size is almost indispensable in the case of copying with SWAP.

As with BACKUP, there are two cases. The case of a copy requiring a swap and the case that does not require a swap. If the 6809 X and Y records are equal, EXTRAMON assumes that the user wants to copy his file to the same drive, but to another diskette and with the same name. In this case, if the buffer zone is not long enough to contain the file, COPY returns the hand with SWPFLG<>0. It is then for the calling program to display a message of the style "PLEASE INSERT DESTINATION etc" and when the user has changed his diskette, COPY1 should be reminded which continues the work. It's over for the calling program when SWPFLG sucks.

If X is different from Y, there is no problem and nothing else to do. Attention you must have booked at least 2 FCBs (see FCBINI) to be able to make COPY.

The NEW-COPY entry point is very close to cCOPYO, the only difference is better memory management. The DEBZON and FINZON pointers are then on 3 bytes, the third being a bank number (between 1 and 6). DEBZON and FINZON define the area used in all cases. In the case of a single-reader copy, the follow-up is by call from COPY1.


===== Page 282 =====
M06 Ext.

5.12 Destroying a file # input point: KILL (14) # input parameters: - DK.DRV (2049H) Drive Number - FILNAM (224FH-2259H) File name, 11 characters. Eight bytes for the name. 3 bytes for extension.

# Return settings

Unless you're wrong, your file is lost (you searched for it).

5.13 Change of file name # entry point: NAME (15)

# Input settings:

- register 6809 Xx Pointe file old name.

- register 6809 Y Pointe file next name. X as Y point to a 20 byte area.

bytes 0-7 Name

bytes 8-10 Extension

bytes 11-18 Comments

Byte 19 Drive Number.

EXTRAMON checks that the new name is not already on the diskette.

5.16 File size # input point: LOF (18) #* input parameters: - FCNUM (2244H) File logical number. #* Return settings:

~ FCC (214EH) Result in whole 16 bits (FACMO (2150H)) or in real 4 depending on size, to know it you have to test VALTYP (2 or 4). In the case of files with direct access, the number of records of the file is returned. In sequence, this is the number of sectors of the file,

Calculation formula for direct access : LOF := ( NBoct * Nbsector - pointless ) / RLEN

With

NBoct = Number of bytes per sector

NBsector = Number of sectors in the file.

Unnecessary = No. of useless bytes of last sector RLEN = Size of record.

6 L'EDITEUR
# point of entry: EDIT (22) #* input parameters

- register 6809 X Address of stamp - register 6809 Y End of buffer (last useful byte) - DEFTXT (21E0H-21E 1H) Point on text replacing keyboard input - IWTFLG (22A94) Rapid output semaphore

The buffer is filled when hitting the ENTER key. The end of the line is recognized by an OOH. The accents are coded with SS2 sequences. Characters not recognized by the editor are deleted on the screen. If the user hits CNT-C, EDIT returns the hand to the application with an empty buffer.

CAUTION: no buffer should be provided < 3 characters,

This routine makes it easy to use a Basic type editor. In entry the caller provides a buffer, output it receives the read line. This editor works in M05 mode, 80 column mode and green on black in 4-color bitmap. It has all the features of the well-known Basic editor. Editing is done in the current window (see PUTC$ and its US sequences to fix this window).

The editor tests (when 11 has nothing else to do) 1 byte IWTFLG, if it becomes different from zero, there is an output of the editor with an empty buffer. This allows an application to take over the hand if necessary, for example: IWTFLG can be changed in an interruption routine, or in the direction of GETC$ monitor, if the application wants to take over a function key,

The editor may be asked to take a text pointed by DEFTXT instead of the keyboard entry, the text ending with an OOH. To not have text by default, you must point DEFTXT on an OOH. Examples: Basic uses it for AUTO, entering the DEFTXT editor points to the line number to display, so this line number is written on the screen as if the user had hit it. Thanks to the indirection of SETC$ (on MO6 by redirecting SWI), the application can point DEFTXT to a text when pressing a function key. F1 ==> RUN F2 ==> LIST etc... In fact, the indirect routine of GETC$ must make the first character ML"er point DEFTXT to IST,OOH. If, for example, there is a CR in the line, it is automatically validated by the editor,

9 UTILISATION DE L'EXTRAMONITEUR SOUS BASIC 128

This chapter only concerns extramon users under Basic 128,

If you use EXTRAMON from EXEC or BASIC equivalents, there are several points to consider:

During an EXEC, BASIC passes over to the user's binary program with the BARK ROM BASIC switched, so the user can call certain standard entry points of the BASIC ROM. If he wishes to call EXTRAMON, 11 must first call the EXTRAMON routine (EFFBH) via a JSR. To be able to repeat calls to the basic ROM, a new call to this routine will recommut the basic ROM.

Be careful, if you want to recover the errors, consider redirecting the error catch (ZPERR (2185H) vector), otherwise the Basic error message will be displayed. To make up for errors, you have to redirect ZPERR to the next program

LDS SAVSTK SAVSTK = $2175
LEAS 3,5                 ; Jump a few bytes
STB 2,8                  ; the caller error code

RTI et                   ; return to the appellant

===== Page 300 =====
GATE ARRAY MODE PAGE MO
AVERTISSEMENT

This document is intended for readers interested in familiarising themselves with the internal operation of THOMSON microcomputers of type MO as well as programmers wishing to directly control the hardware of the machines or simply wishing to better understand the course of the provided routines

In ROM monitor.

Only the standard input points of the monitor ROMs that are supplied with each machine have guaranteed operation and direct compatibility from one model to another. Any software that directly exploits the hardware resources of the machine - including the circuit described in these pages - is subject to a loss of compatibility as new models are released.

Under no circumstances is it guaranteed that the operation of the circuit described below will be maintained in its current version.

I - PRESENTATION

Since the MOS, the central units of our machines are built around a "Gate Array" circuit, named after the technology used, which provides the essential electronic functions of the system:

- refreshment and access to memory,

- video management,

- light pen...

This circuit is designed in such a way that, when the CPU accesses the screen memory, its access is directed either to the "mark" memory also called "form" memory, or to the "color" memory, depending on the FORME bit driven by the software; The automatic scanning circuits of the screen memory - contained in the Gate Array - simultaneously access the "forms" memory and the "colors" memory, regardless of the value of the FORME bit, in order to be able to develop in real time the entire visualisation signals intended for the display cathode tube:

fig. 1 CPU bit FORM Memory FORMS Memory Colors Display Circuitry TV or Monitor

For each group of 8 pixels displayed on the screen, the video circuitry must read 16 bits in memory:

- 8 bits for the form byte and

- 8 bits for the "color" byte

Until now, this reading had to be done in one single, 16-bit, which required the video memory to be physically organized into two 8-bit benches, while all the rest of the system memory is only 8-bit. This "irregular" organisation is not economically optimal and does not support the use of N memory boxes

X 1 bits which are yet the most widespread.

In order to remedy this disadvantage, the new Gate Array, called "Page Mode", replaces 16-bit access to video memory with two successive accesses


===== Page 301 =====
8 bits very fast that total the same duration as the old 16 bit access. This technique is made possible thanks to a particular address mode of dynamic memories called "Page" mode in which several memory boxes of the component can be read in sequence provided that these boxes all belong to the same row of the internal matrix. This is how we organize video memory so that the "form" and "color" bytes are accessible in Page mode. Obviously, when viewed from the software, the screen organization remains unchanged and compatible with the previous system, i.e. the bit FORME always allows you to choose the CPU access, either to the "form" memory or to the "color" memory.

In addition to this new feature - which remains transparent to the software - this new Gate Array incorporates additional features that allow to manage more memory and produce the various display modes already available on the TOS.

Like other devices, the Gate Array Mode Page is seen from the software as a set of records decoded in the memory space of 6809. The location of these records in the address area of 6809 is fixed because they are coded inside the Gate Array itself. Depending on whether the Gate Array is used in an MO system or an TO system, the address of its records is different:

- in MO system, the 6 registers are located in :
ATE4, ATES, A7E6, A7E7, A7DC, A7DD

A complete summary of the assignment of these records is given at the end of this chapter. Until then, this document examines one by one the functions of the circuit.

II - MANAGEMENT OF LIVING MEMORY

The Gate Array Mode Page allows you to manage up to 512 K of RAM memory per 16 K pages. These physical pages of living memory, whose number depends on the central unit under consideration and the possible memory extension, are

affectable to several logical spaces of the system. As in the TO7-70 and

the TOS is a "bank-switch" system that allows the 6809 to access

All this memory.

This system has been extended to all the memory space (outside peripherals) of machines in order to increase the flexibility of the environment. This extension led to the determination of 4 distinct logical address spaces:

"Scran" space

the "RAM system" space (or fixed page) the "RAM Data" space

"Cartouche" space

SYSTEME MO
fig. 2
FFFF F000 EFFF B000 AFFF A000 SFFF 6000
5FFF 2000 1FFF 0000 Monitor Cartridge I/O RAM Data RAM fixed (system) Screen (bit FORM=0) Screen

(bit FORM=1) N 16 K physical pages (up to 32 pages)


===== Page 302 =====
31 2 1 0

Physical pages can be assigned to these logical spaces by programming Gate Array registers, the pages being simply identified by their number.

The individual behavior of each logical space is specified in the following.

II - 1 - FIXED RAM SPACE

This logical space containing vital information to the system (such as the monitor's zero page), is not reconfigurable and it is the physical page 1 of the memory that is affected.

There is no registry to re-program this space.

II - 2 - CLEAR SPACE

This space contains the information used by the video playback controller to synthesize the displayed image. By default, the physical page 0 is displayed, but it is possible to ask the controller to display pages 1, 2, or 3.

The operation is as follows:

- after a Reset, a power-up or if the D7, D6 bits of the Gate Array registry located in A7DD are both 0, the physical page 0 is displayed on the screen. The way the data is interpreted by the display controller depends on the display mode chosen (see paragraph IV-1).

- by reprogramming the D7D6 bits of A7DD, you can display physical pages 1, 2 or 3. If we ask for the display of page 1, it is the

contents of the FIXED RAM space that will appear on the screen. This content has little interest, and it is mainly pages 2 and 3 that are usable.

- when the CPU accesses the ECRAN logical space (0 to 1FFF), it is ALWAYS the physical page 0 that is addressed, even if the display controller uses another page (D7D6 of A7DD # 00). In ECRAN's logical space, the CPU always uses the FORM bit to select the FORMS or COLOURS memory (bit 0 of A7CO0).

- if the CPU wants to access pages 2 and 3 by considering them as screens, it must assign them to its RAM DATA space (6000 to 9FFF) in order to read or write them. In this case, the memory corresponding to the "forms" should be considered to be at the "top" addresses of the page while the "low" addresses contain the "color" information; Indeed, the traditional FORME bit is inoperative in the DATA space.

6000 - 7FFF = 8000 colour plan - 9FFF = "form" plan

- the page displayed by the video controller can be simultaneously assigned to the DATA space and even to the CARTOUCHE space.


===== Page 303 =====
Here are some examples of configuration:

fig. 4 CPU RAM DATA space page 4 ECRAN (bit FORM=0) ECRAN (bit FORM=1) page 0 Display automation

D7D6 de A7DD = 00
In this standard configuration, the screen consists of the physical page 0 that the CPU accesses through the screen logical space.

This mode is the only standardized mode; it is operated by monitor display routines such as PUTCH.

fig. 5

CPU RAM DATA space ECRAN space page 4 page 2 page 0 A7DD D7D6 display controller = 10

Page 2 is displayed but the CPU cannot access it since the ECRAN space necessarily leads to page 0 and it is page 4 that is assigned to

RAM DATA space.

fig. 6

CPU RAM DATA Space ECRAN Space page 2 page 0

A7DD D7D6 display controller = 10

Page 2 is displayed and the CPU can update it through space

RAM DATA logic. The CPU always has access to page 0 through the ECRAN logical space.

Standard monitor display routines are inoperative in such a configuration.

II - 3 - SPACE RAM DATA

This logical space, located between 6000 and 9FFF is used by the

applications for their own needs and data. Already on TO7-70 and on TO9, this space was managed in "bank-switch" using PIA bits which allowed to select a 16 K physical page from 6.

The "bank switch" mechanism implemented in the Gate Array is simple, since just write the desired physical page number (from 0 to 31) in the D4-D0 bits of the Gate Array A7E5 registry so that the relevant page is assigned to the RAM DATA space.

Figure 7

ATES D4 - DO


===== Page 304 =====
NE 0,..., 31 RAM DATA Page N

Thus, memory management is simplified as a simple physical page number entry is enough to affect this page.

The number of pages available will depend on the central unit, its possible extensibility in memory by an extension module. Gate Array does not itself provide an indication of the number of pages; Therefore, the monitor indicates the number of the last physical page that can be used in the DERBAN register on page zero. Refer to the monitor documentation.

In order to reproduce the standard environment, you must assign page 2 to the RAM DATA space; Indeed, in Reset and for hardware questions, the content of the 5 bit D4-D0 of A7E5 is 0, which affects the physical page 0 normally reserved for the screen.

II - 4 - CARTRIDGE SPACE

This space, located between B000 and EFFF, normally contains ROM in the form of external cartridge or internal memory in the system containing BASIC 1, monitor, or other BASIC.

It is nevertheless possible to assign RAM RAM to this logical space by putting the DS bit of the Gate Array A7E6 registry to 1.

The RAM memory management assigned to the cartridge space depends on the bit D6 value of the A7E7 registry; This bit selects one of the following two management systems:

- the direct log mode: as with the management of the RAM DATA space, this mode allows you to choose the physical page assigned to the CARTOUCHE logical space by writing directly its 5 bit number in

Gate Array Register A7E6.

fig. 8
ATE D6 D5 D4
DO 1 N space CARTOUCHE page N

- Nano-Network compatible mode (LEANORD registered mark): this system allows you to fully simulate the RAM 64 K THOMSON RAM 64 K NR 5 cartridge for MO5 developed within the Nano-Network. In this mode, the programming of the RAM cartridge by the A7CB register

is "reproduced" so as to ensure compatibility of software created for

MO5. The 4 16 K pages of the RAM cartridge for MO5 are replaced by pages 4 to 7. Only 2 bits (D1D0) of A7CB being assigned to the

page on the nano-network; So there is a transposition of numbers 0 to 3 programmed into A7CB to lead to physical pages 4 to 7.

fig. 9
ATET D6 0 ATCB D3 D2 DI1 DO

WE RAM/ROM 1 0 CARTOUCHE space page 7 page 6


===== Page 305 =====
page 5 page 4 fig. 10

In this mode, the contents of the A7E6 register are actually a transposition of the A7CB register:

ATCB WE RAM/ROM ATE6 WE RAM/ ROM 0 0

At Reset or powering, the nano-network compatible mode is taken, i.e. the A7E7 bit D6 is 0.

Whether the management is done directly by the A7E6 register or by the

Register A7CB (Nano-Network mode), a Write Enable (WE) programming bit allows you to ban or allow writing in the RAM physical page assigned to the CARTOUCHE space. This gives protection against

Untimely writings, memory behaves like ROM.

In summary, the physical pages of live memory are affectable 4 the ECRAN space, the RAM DATA space or the CARTOUCHE space.

- the ECRAN space whose display is managed by A7DD,

- the RAM DATA space is managed by the A7E5 registry,

- the CARTOUCHE space is managed either by the A7E6 register or by the A7CB register in Nano-Network compatible MO mode.

The CARTOUCHE space between B000 and EFFF has a particular behavior, already present on the MO5. Indeed, if one divides by thought the 16 K CARTOUCHE space into four subspaces of 4 K each, these 4 parts do not present a direct correspondence between their logical address (by the 4 consecutive subspaces B000-BFFF, C000-CFFF, DO00-DFFF, E000-EFFF) and their physical address (4 physical pages of 4 K consecutive).

The correspondence is as follows:

Logic Address Physical Address BO000-BFFF 4th page of 4 K (high addresses) C000-CFFF 4K page (low addresses) D000-DFFF 2nd page of 4 K E000-EFFF 3rd page of 4 K

This logical/physical organization is also found in the ROM BASIC 128 of MO systems.

This has an important influence on the use of RAM in the CARTOUCHE space, especially if you transfer the information through the RAM DATA space to put it in the CARTOUCHE space. Indeed, the RAM DATA space itself has a logical/physical assignment within each page

16 K. This assignment has no importance or effect

until the RAM DATA space information is copied to space


===== Page 306 =====
CARTOUCHE or vice versa, but account must be taken of the transposition resulting from such a transfer.

The correspondence between logical address and physical address within the RAM DATA space is as follows:

The 16 K page is considered to be divided into four 4 K subspaces:

Log address Physical address 6000-6FFF 3rd page of 4 K 7000-7FFF 4th page of 4 K

(high addresses)

8000-8FFF next page of 4 K (low addresses)

9000-9FFF 2nd page of 4 K

Therefore, the transposition between RAM DATA and CARTOUCHE gives the following table:

RAM DATA Space CARTOUCHE Space
6000-6FFF E000-EFFF
7000-7FFF BO000-BFFF
8000-8FFF C000-CFFF
9000-9FFF D000-DFFF

This correspondence is bijective.

The correspondence between the other logical spaces can also be mentioned.

The correspondence between RAM DATA and ECRAN has already been given in paragraph II-2:

RAM DATA space ECRAN space 6000-7FFF Plan Color 8000-9FFF Plan Shape

The correspondence between CARTOUCHE and ECRAN is therefore:

Espace CARTOUCHE Espace ECRAN B000-BFFF 2nd half of the Plan Color C000-CFFF 1st half of the Plan Shape D000-DFFF 2nd half of the Plan Shape

E000-EFFF


===== Page 307 =====
This correspondence must be used by any programmer wishing to prepare a screen in the CARTOUCHE space. The other half (low addresses) of a Plan (Form or Color) corresponds to the upper half of the screen,

the second half (high addresses) corresponding to the lower part of the screen. The actual meaning of Shape and Color plans depends on the mode of display.

III - DISPLAY MANAGEMENT

The Gate Array Mode Page incorporates the entire video circuitry capable of managing the different display modes of the TO9. It is intended to drive an external pallet directly. The monitor's routines allow you to manage the different modes. No direct programming of the circuit shall be undertaken.

IV - LIGHT PEN MANAGEMENT

The Gate Array Mode Page contains the circuitry associated with light-pen allowing you to measure with pixel resolution. Software management is carried out by monitor routines. No direct programming of the circuit shall be undertaken.

V - GENERAL ORGANISATION OF REGISTERS

This paragraph summarizes all the functions of the 6 Gate Array registries.

VI - 1 - SYSTEM 1 REGISTER (A7E7)

This register can only be written.

WRITING
D7 D6 D5 D4 D3 D2 D1 DO
ATET reserved management reserved management reserved RAM in RAM in

the ARTOUCHE space the DATA space

D6=0 Nano-Network compatible mode

D6 = 1 management by

internal register A7E6

This register cannot be read again and only certain bits can be modified by the user, so the copy of this register managed by the monitor under the name MA7E7 must be used. This copy should be read and the necessary bits modified before writing to the Gate Array registry. This copy must ALWAYS be kept up to date so as not to disrupt the monitor's operation.


===== Page 308 =====
VI - 2 - SYSTEM 2 REGISTER (A7DD)

WRITING
D7 D6 D5 D4 D3 D2 D1 DO
ATDD page number RAM displayed R ES E R V E

D7D6 = page number RAM physics displayed by the video controller

We can't read anything in A7DD. Nevertheless, the D7D6 bits are rereadable in the A7E4 register (see paragraph VI - 4).

VI - 3 - DISPLAY REGISTER (A7DC)

This register is reserved for the use of the monitor.

VI - 4 - LIGHT-PEN/SYSTEM 2 REGISTER (AT7E4)

WRITING
D7 D6 D5 D4 D3 D2 D1 DO
ATE4 0 0 0 0 0 0 reserved selection reserved reserved light-pen/registries

The 0 bits are reserved for future extensions and must not be set to 1 to maintain compatibility.

DO = 0 readable access to the "CARTOUCHE" and "RAM DATA" registers and system part 2 of this A7E4 register.

DO = 1 readable access to light-pen records. Light-pen interruption allowed.

The value that was written in the DO bit of A7E4 can be read again in the DO bit of System Registry 1 (A7ET7).

On reading, the A7E4 register can have two values, according to the DO bit that was written in A7TE4:

- if DO of A7E4 was written to 0, then rereading A7E4 provides copies of some bits of system registry 2.


===== Page 309 =====
10

- if DO of A7E4 was written to 1, then rereading A7E4 provides light-pen information that is reserved for the use of the monitor.

READING * DO of A7E4 writes to 0:
D7 D6 D5 D4 D3 D2 D1 DO
ATE4 page number reserved 0 0 0 0

RAM displayed re-read D7D6 bits written in the "system 2" register in A7DD

* DO of A7E4 written to 1: light-pen information.

(This register is only available in writing if the A7E7 bit D4 is written at 1)

ATES reserved 0 0 RAM page number used in space
RAM DATA

On reading, the A7E5 register can have two values, depending on the DO bit that was written in A7E4:

- if DO of A7E4 was written to 0, then reading A7E5 returns the physical page number assigned to RAM DATA logical space,

- if DO of A7E4 was written to 1, then reading A7E5 gives information about the light-pen reserved for the monitor.
